The page displays a circular alchemical emblem, printed in color and affixed to the paper. A white outer ring contains the title **Hermetic Philosophy** inscribed in gold capital letters. 

Within this ring is a blue oval field bordered by a green <term>Ouroboros (an ancient symbol of a serpent biting its own tail, representing the eternal cycle of life, death, and the unity of all things)</term>. At the top of the blue field, a golden sunburst radiates light. 

Below the sun, a white pelican is shown "in its piety" <note>a traditional heraldic and alchemical image of a mother bird wounding her own breast to feed her young with her blood, symbolizing self-sacrifice and the process of spiritual or material refinement</note> atop a nest. This nest rests upon a quartered shield, where each section contains a rose of brown and gold <note>the presence of the rose and shield suggests a connection to the Rosicrucian tradition, a philosophical secret society that blended mysticism with alchemy</note>.

<summary>This frontispiece presents a symbolic emblem titled "Hermetic Philosophy," utilizing classic alchemical and Rosicrucian imagery such as the Ouroboros and the self-sacrificing pelican. It serves as a decorative and meaningful visual opening to the manuscript, signaling its focus on hidden wisdom and the "Great Work" of alchemy.</summary>

# PREFACE
->To the Christian<-
## R E A D E R.

IF you, dear Reader, are so fortunate that your blind eyes have been opened to know your deep misery <note>In the Dutch Reformed tradition, "misery" (elende) refers to the essential first step of recognizing one’s sinful state before God, as outlined in the Heidelberg Catechism.</note> with the result that through faith you have taken refuge in the Desire of all Nations <note>original: "wenſen aller Heydenen." This is a traditional title for Jesus Christ, derived from the biblical book of Haggai 2:7.</note>, and in that Lord in whom there is righteousness and strength <note>original: "geregtigheden en ſterkten." A reference to Isaiah 45:24, indicating that the believer finds both legal standing and moral power in Christ alone.</note>; then you can consider nothing else your greatest interest <term>interest: In 17th-century religious writing, one's "interest" (intereſt) refers to their personal spiritual stake or "portion" in Christ and eternal salvation.</term> than that you might obtain, through the use of all means, the joyful awareness of the truth and sincerity of your faith; for on the strong

The primary goal of this following little **treatise** <term>treatise: Translated from "Tractaatje," meaning a small book or pamphlet, typically one dealing with a religious or moral subject.</term> is to assist you in reaching a well-founded **certainty** <term>certainty: From "vaſtſtelling," referring here to the spiritual assurance or "establishment" of one's standing in grace.</term> regarding your state, and toward an **encouraged walk** <term>encouraged walk: From "gemoedigden wandel," describing a Christian life conducted with a courageous, peaceful, and spirited heart before God.</term> before the Lord. <image-desc>A decorative rectangular woodcut headpiece at the top of the page features a central rose surrounded by symmetrical scrolling foliage and floral motifs. ## Dear Christians.

It is my conviction that if believers had a more thorough knowledge of the **Gospel truths** <note>original: "Evangelische waarheden"; refers to the core teachings of the New Testament</note> and made more frequent use of them, they would be better able—following the example of the **first Apostolic Church**—to walk in the **fear of the Lord** <note>original: "vreeze des Heeren"; a term for deep, reverent awe and obedience toward God</note> (which is the essence of practical holiness in life) and in the comfort of the **Holy Spirit** <note>Likely a typographical error for "Geests" (Spirit), as it refers to Acts 9:31.</note>, and thus possess peace. **Acts 9:31**. If this were so, there would not be so much cause to complain about **God’s desertions** <note>original: "Gods verlaatingen"; a theological term for the feeling that God has withdrawn his presence or favor</note>, nor about the meager distribution of sanctifying and comforting **grace**. I wish now to prove this in detail; and to that end, I shall do two things:

First, I will present several principal **Foundations** <note>original: "Gronden"; the fundamental theological principles on which faith rests</note> upon which a soul, who takes refuge in Christ according to the instruction of the Gospel, can and must conclude in an infallible manner that all comfort, holiness, and salvation is laid up for her and is certainly to be expected. This is intended to prevent, cut off, or suppress all doubts regarding her good standing and her progress in the same.

Secondly, I will give some specific **cautionary directions** <note>original: "Waarschouwende bestieringen"; practical guidance or instructions intended to warn against spiritual pitfalls</note> for the purpose of—

<meta>catchword: that</meta>

<summary>The author begins an address to the reader, arguing that a deeper understanding of the Gospel leads to a life of holiness and peace like that of the early Church. He proposes a two-part study: establishing the foundations of a believer's security in Christ and providing practical spiritual guidance.</summary>

<keywords>Gospel truths, holiness, Holy Spirit, Acts 9:31, divine grace, spiritual doubt, foundations of faith, Christian life</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's introductory address. The previous page ended with the author's intention to explain the "foundations" (gronden) upon which a believer can find comfort in Christ. This page begins by completing that thought and then categorizing those who are currently excluded from such spiritual comfort due to ignorance or lack of faith.</meta>

# Nature and Foundations
<meta>catchword: of</meta>

...that every sincere soul might be made capable of using those **foundations** <note>original: "gronden"; the basic principles of faith upon which spiritual peace is built</note> and never be kept from them.

In order to arrive at the first point, it will be necessary for us to put forward the following things, which I shall nevertheless have to clarify in a few words so that they may be beyond dispute; and thus I will pave the way to be able to claim and use all those **grounds of comfort** <note>original: "troostgronden"</note> without hindrance, for the sake of comfort and holiness.

The first thing I put forward is that four types of people have no ground to expect any comfort or grace in the state in which they are found; namely:

1. Such people who have no knowledge of the first **fundamental truths** <note>original: "grondtwaarheden"</note> that one must know in order to deal with God regarding the state of one’s soul; namely: that all people are **sinners** <note>original: "zondaars"</note>, enemies of God by nature, and therefore lying under God’s curse and wrath; that souls do not die, but that on the day of the general judgment they shall be reunited with their bodies; that every person will have an eternal fate, either in hell or in heaven; That Christ is the **Mediator** <note>original: "Middelaar"; a theological title for Jesus Christ as the bridge between God and humanity</note>, sent and capable of making **reconciliation** <note>original: "verzoening"; the process of restoring a broken relationship between God and people</note> with God; that faith and holiness are required as the way and the means to come to the enjoyment of God; and other such necessary truths: these people are far from comfort.

2. Such people have no ground to expect comfort or grace, who, whatever they...

<summary>The author identifies that spiritual comfort is only available to those who understand core Christian doctrines, listing the "fundamental truths" of the human condition and Christ's role. He begins to categorize groups of people who are currently excluded from this grace, starting with the ignorant.</summary>
<keywords>foundations of faith, grounds of comfort, Mediator, reconciliation, fundamental truths, spiritual state, judgment, salvation</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s introductory discourse on the "foundations of comfort." The previous page listed the first category of people who cannot claim spiritual comfort: those ignorant of basic truths like the nature of sin and Christ’s role as Mediator. This page continues that list, identifying three more categories of people who are excluded from grace due to their lack of repentance or unwillingness to change.</meta>

### of Faith.

...of the mentioned **fundamental truths** <note>original: "grondt-waarheden"; the core doctrines of the Christian faith</note>, yet do not believe nor acknowledge that they are **death-guilty** <note>deserving of death as a divine penalty for sin</note> and damnable people, against whom the curse in the **Law** <note>original: "Wet"; the biblical moral law that condemns sin</note> is pronounced; who cannot believe, nor imagine, that they have so corrupted themselves through sins that God the Lord would justly damn them for their sins; all **grounds of comfort** <note>original: "troostgronden"</note> are closed to them.

3. Such have no ground to expect comfort or **grace** <note>original: "genade"</note>, who although they acknowledge themselves worthy of damnation, and indeed wish to be delivered from their wretched and lost state, nevertheless want to hold fast to their sins, and not break the covenant with the same, but willfully keep their sins at hand, not wanting the same killed or brought under: These people do not want to be in the hand of the **Master-Physician** <note>original: "Medecynmeester"; a traditional metaphor for Christ as the healer of the soul</note>, they do not want to be healed, and therefore not be saved; they are then willful rejecters of grace: and therefore, instead of comfort and grace, **God’s wrath** <note>original: "Gods toorn"</note>, the curses of the Law, and hellish damnation must be applied and brought to their hearts, to the end that they might no longer remain unwilling to be rescued from their miserable, lost state.

4. Such people also have not the least ground to expect comfort, or grace, or salvation, who although they confess their sinfulness and wretchedness, and declare a desire to be released not only from their debts but also from their clinging sins and defects, nevertheless do not want to [enter] that way—

<meta>catchword: flaan/ (completing the word "inslaan," meaning to strike or enter a path)</meta>

<summary>This page identifies the categories of people who cannot claim spiritual comfort: those who deny their own sinfulness, those who refuse to give up their sins despite wanting salvation, and those unwilling to follow the prescribed path of faith.</summary>
<keywords>repentance, Law, God's wrath, Master-Physician, fundamental truths, grace, damnation, spiritual healing</keywords>

# Nature and Grounds

...[re]sist <note>original: "staan"; likely the conclusion of "wederstaan" (to resist) or "tegenstaan" (to oppose) from the previous page</note> / the way which the **Gospel** <note>original: "Evangelium"</note> presents / but wish to be justified through their own works / entirely or in part / or [wish to] receive forgiveness through **God’s** mercy / without **Christ's** satisfaction <note>original: "Christi voldoening"; the theological doctrine that Christ's suffering and death paid the debt of sin to divine justice</note> / or wish to practice virtues and duties and improve their faults through their own strength. Through these, there is no ground for comfort because they still, entirely or in part, intentionally reject the only means / and the only way of comfort and salvation / not yet recognizing their helplessness / and lost state / nor wishing to forsake the way of the **covenant of works** <note>original: "verbond der werken"; the legalistic belief that one can be saved by perfectly obeying God's laws</note> / in order to be saved through the way of free and all-sufficient grace.

These four types of persons I then set aside / with whom I now wish to have nothing to do / as they are in no state / to receive any assurance of comfort or peace or grace; whoever is such / who reads this / let them know / that I can produce no ground of comfort for them from the entire word of **God**. Accordingly, this leads to:

The second [point] that I put forward, is / that in all those / to whom firm grounds of comfort would be presented / must be found the true faith / which unites the soul with **Christ** / and takes pleasure / in being saved according to **God’s** decree / and being brought to salvation <note>original: "zaligheid"</note>. I require only this single grace in the souls / to whom I shall speak; and without that, there is no speaking to anyone’s assurance / that all grace and salvation shall come to them.

The third point put forward is / that the nature

<summary>The author identifies legalists and those relying on self-improvement as the final group excluded from divine comfort, before establishing that true faith is the essential prerequisite for any soul to receive the assurance of salvation.</summary>

<meta>This page continues from page 4, where the author began discussing the prerequisites for spiritual comfort. Having excluded those who rely on their own works, the author now begins to define the "nature and essence" of true, saving faith, specifically arguing what it is NOT.</meta>

->of Faith<-

...[na]ture <note>original: "tuur"; the conclusion of "natuur" (nature) from the previous page</note> and the essence of **saving faith** <note>original: "zaligmakende geloof"; faith that leads to salvation</note> does not consist in one of these two things.

1. Not in an assurance / or certain knowledge and trust / that **Christ**, for example / has died for me; that he is my savior / that I shall have life / and salvation through him; faith is no mere persuasion <note>original: "overredentheyd"</note> / nor a sweet imaginary assurance / that my sins are forgiven / that **God** loves me / and is my **God** and Father for **Christ’s** sake <note>original: "om Christi wil"</note>: For 1. many a believer has no assurance that **Christ** is theirs / so that there would be believers / without faith / which is a contradiction. 2. Many a self-deceived hypocrite <note>original: "huychelaar"</note> has assurance / and personal application / that **Christ** died for him / that his sins are forgiven, etc. / and yet he has no true faith; thus, assurance is not the essence of faith. 3. Believing in the Lord **Jesus** is a duty of all who are under the **Gospel** <note>original: "Evangelium"</note> / and the Teachers <note>original: "Leeraars"; the ministers or pastors of the church</note> / must insist upon it to everyone as a command / and he who does not believe in **Christ** / sins heavily; Now, to take up assurance and firm trust / that **Christ** died for us / is not the duty of all / who hear the **Gospel** / for that is a lie / and no one must believe a lie / and a Teacher must insist / that no one believe a lie; namely this / that **Christ** died for all those / to whom the **Gospel** is offered; thus, the nature and the essence of true faith consists not in an assurance or certain trust, that also to me the forgiveness of sins, the eter[nal]- <note>original: "eeuwi-"; the word is completed on the next page as "eeuwige" (eternal)</note>

<meta>The catchword "ge" indicates the start of the next word "eeuwige" (eternal) on the following page.</meta>

<summary>The author argues that the essence of saving faith is not the same as the personal assurance of salvation. He contends that many true believers lack assurance, while many hypocrites falsely possess it, and cautions that ministers should not command everyone to believe Christ died specifically for them, as that may not be true for all listeners.</summary>
<keywords>saving faith, assurance, Christ, Gospel, salvation, hypocrite, pastoral duty, atonement</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of insufficient definitions of faith. # Nature and Foundations

righteousness <note>original: "geregtigheid"; in a theological sense, this refers to being made "right" or "just" before God.</note> and Salvation, is given by God out of pure grace, for *Christ’s* sake (as the *Catechism* <note>The author refers to the Heidelberg Catechism, a foundational teaching document for the Dutch Reformed Church.</note> says) or as the form of the *Holy Supper* <note>The "Formulier van 't H. Avontmaal" is the official liturgical text read during the celebration of Communion.</note> says: that it would be a faith of this certain promise of God, that all my sins are forgiven me only for the sake of the suffering and death of *Jesus Christ*, and that the perfect righteousness of *Christ* is <term>imputed<gloss>credited or assigned to one’s spiritual account rather than earned</gloss></term> and granted to me as my own, yes, so perfectly, etc.

2. Faith does not consist in an act of the understanding alone; I say alone, or only, without the person going further and exercising an act of the will or of the heart; I say, faith is not merely a consent of the mind or of the understanding to all the revealed truths of God as infallible truths, and especially to the *Evangelical* <note>Refers to the "Gospel" (Evangelium) or the "Good News" of the New Testament.</note> truths, that God in love and grace wants to forgive sinners and bring life through the blood and mediator *Jesus Christ*; although this intellectual consent is also absolutely required, as without which no *Evangelical* and saving faith is, or can be: for it is like the root and fundamental ground of all that the soul does through faith. However, the devils also accept those truths of the *Gospel* as all Divine and infallible truths, as appears in *Mark* 1:24. *Mark* 5:2, 6, 7, 10, 12. *Luke* 8:28, 31, 32. *Matthew* 8:28, 31. *James* 2:19. and so also many <term>temporary believers<gloss>original: "tijd geloovigen"; people who appear to believe for a time but lack a deep, lasting spiritual root</gloss></term> do, *John* 1:23, 24. *John* 8:31, 33, 34. *Acts* 8:13. and yet they lack saving faith; to which I add these reasons. 1. --- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s discussion on the nature of faith, moving from the human act of the will to the divine foundation of the plan of salvation. It establishes that the way of salvation was designed to bring glory to God’s various attributes.</meta>

# Nature and Foundations.

I will only say this beforehand / as the foundation of faith / that God has in His eternal wisdom, love, and grace planned / devised / firmly established / and revealed in His word a way of life and salvation / namely / that lost, sinful, powerless, and miserable people should be saved through *Christ* / and by free grace: and in this way of salvation, God has in the first place intended His own glory / namely:

1. The glory of His justice / the justice of God is revealed from faith to faith, *Romans 1: 17.* <note>The author quotes the New Testament frequently to ground his theological claims in scripture.</note> we are, says the *Apostle* <note>Refers to the Apostle Paul.</note> / justified freely by His grace, through the redemption that is in *Christ Jesus*, whom God has set forth to be a propitiation <note>A sacrifice that regains favor or provides reconciliation.</note> through faith in His blood to show (<note>NB</note> <note>original: "NB"; stands for *Nota Bene*, meaning "Note Well."</note>) His justice. *Romans 3: 24/ 25/ 26.*

2. The glory of His love / His infinite love for humanity; for God so loved the world, *1 John 3: 16.* <note>The text cites 1 John 3:16, though "God so loved the world" is more famously found in John 3:16.</note> Hereby have we known love, that He laid down His life for us, *1 John 3: 16.* In this was the love of God toward us revealed, that God sent His only begotten Son into the world, as a propitiation for our sins, that we might live through Him. *1 John 4: 9/ 10.* *Titus 3: 4/ 5.* *Ephesians 2: 4/ 5.*

3. The glory of His grace and mercy; for God has made us accepted <note>original: "begenadigt"; literally "graced" or "endowed with grace."</note> in the beloved, to the praise of the glory of His grace, *Ephesians 1: 5/ 6.*

## Nature and Grounds
<header>Nature and Grounds</header>

Embracing and consent; just as it is called an embracing of the promises and a kissing of the Son. **Heb. 11:13. Ps. 2:12.** <note>The author refers to the practice of "kissing the Son" as an act of submission and adoration common in ancient Near Eastern royalty.</note> Furthermore, this consent is expressed by buying from **Christ** all that is necessary: wine and milk, gold tested by fire, white garments, and eye salve. **Isa. 55:1. Rev. 3:18. Matt. 13:44.** This buying is nothing else than a willingness—and rightly so—that everything should come for nothing <note>The original "om niet" means "without cost" or "by grace alone," emphasizing that the believer brings no payment.</note> through the hand of the **Mediator**; for we bring no price nor worthiness; the same is expressed by the opening of one’s heart and the wide opening of the mouth, so that **Jesus** with his spirit may enter into our souls and take possession of them, and that we may have fellowship with him and share in his goods, and he with us. **Ps. 81:11. Rev. 3:20. Ps. 24:6 / 7.** This also is nothing else than a heartfelt consent to **Jesus's** knocking through his word and spirit.

Again, the faithful receiving and embracing is called in the **Scripture**: giving the hand to God or to **Christ** <note>original: "de handt geven"; this signifies a gesture of surrender or entering into a solemn alliance/covenant.</note> and writing with the hand and sealing that one desires to be the Lord’s. **2 Chron. 30:8. Isa. 44:5. Neh. 9:38.** Or a grasping and holding fast of him, so that he may deliver us, help us, and be everything to us. **Heb. 6:18. Heb. 11:27. Song of Sol. 3:4. Phil. 3:14.** Or clinging fast to him. **1 Cor. 6:17. Ps. 63:9.** Which then is also a simple act of the will. Elsewhere it is called an entrusting of oneself to the **Lord Jesus**. **2 Tim. 1:12.** A trusting, leaning, and supporting upon him. **Isa. 50:10. Ps. 125:1. Ps. 2:12. Song of Sol. 8:5. Isa. 26:1 / 2. Jer. 17:7.** Or a standing upon the Lord...

...a standing in the faith, **Philippians 4:1, 1 Corinthians 16:13**, or a building upon **Christ** as upon a stone or cornerstone. **1 Peter 2:4, 6; Isaiah 28:16.** Finally, believing is expressed by waiting and hoping for the **Lord**, with the promise of not being put to shame, but of being blessed. **Isaiah 49:23; Psalm 146:5; Psalm 7:5** <note>The author's citation of Psalm 7:5 likely refers to the trust in God's deliverance; note that standard modern references for 'hoping in the Lord' often point to Psalm 37:5 or 130:5.</note>; **Isaiah 28:16; Isaiah 30:18; Romans 9:33; Job 13:15; Psalm 147:11; Proverbs 16:20.**

I have wanted and needed to present this so extensively on purpose, so that everyone may have a clear understanding of **Gospel faith**—that is, of the receiving and embracing of the **Gospel**, and of **Christ** in the **Gospel**, and of **Christ** as offered in the **Gospel**. For I know, and have experienced in every way, that almost all believers are ready and accustomed to making incorrect descriptions, conceptions, and expressions of **justifying** and **saving faith**, and of its acts and workings. This then causes great darkness, confusion, and doubt in the heart, so that one does not rightly know how to practice that act of **clinging faith** <note>original: "aanklevenden geloofs"; this refers to a faith that 'sticks' or 'cleaves' to Christ, emphasizing tenacity over emotional feeling.</note> regarding **Jesus**, nor how to help, support, and comfort oneself with that act of faith.

The fifth thing I wanted to set forth is that to practice that act of **saving faith**, and to take notice of it in one's conscience, is not difficult but easy for any person who has the "root of the matter" <note>A phrase from Job 19:28, often used by historical theologians to describe the essential spark of true spiritual life in the soul.</note> within them, and is touched and drawn by **God’s Spirit**, even though they may be very weak and deficient in the matter of **sanctification** <term>sanctification: the lifelong process of becoming more holy and growing in Christian character</term>. in sincerity to declare of himself that he does so, as far as he knows of himself.

But against this, there are two objections that must be resolved, namely:

**First.** It will be said that many of the graced <term>the graced: those who have received God's saving grace; believers.</term> believe they experience the opposite, and say that it is difficult for them to believe in *Christ*, and that they can neither bend nor lift their hearts toward it. To this, I answer the following:

1. **One** must not immediately regard all who complain as being among the graced. Many of them lack a willingness to part from their sins, and thus they cannot be willing to be delivered from their sins through the way of the *Gospel* <note>original: "Evangelium"</note> while they currently walk in those sins with the consent of their hearts.

2. **Many** of the graced understand the "believing acceptance and embracing of *Jesus*" and the "way of the *Gospel*" to mean a sense of assurance and the quiet confidence of the mind, or the feeling of rest, peace, help, and the like. And that is not so easy, as I have previously stated; this misconception causes them to speak of the difficulty of believing.

3. **Even** if many of the graced find within themselves that hearty consent and approval to be helped by *Jesus*—according to the presentation of the *Gospel*—toward peace, holiness, and glory, they still do not wish to call that "believing," precisely because it comes so easily to them. They think that believing must be something else entirely, and so they torment themselves through...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of God's eighth title—the forgiveness of all types of sin—emphasizing that God's own reputation is the guarantee of this mercy. It then transitions into a scholarly critique of the ninth title of God's Name found in Exodus 34:7, arguing that common translations may mistakenly introduce a tone of severity that contradicts God's promised goodness.</meta>

->of Faith.<-

is a **God** who forgives iniquity, transgression, and sin; the respect and honor of this His **Name** serves as assurance and peace of mind for such believing sinners, and the **Lord** binds Himself to this through His **Name**; *I write to you, little children,* says **John**, *because your sins are forgiven you for His name's sake.* **1 John 2:12.**

The ninth Title of **God's** Name of Honor <term>Eernaam: A title or name that reflects one's essential character and reputation</term> is in the following words: *who by no means clears the guilty, visiting the iniquity of the **Fathers** upon the **Children**, and upon the children's children unto the third and fourth generation.* <note>The author is quoting Exodus 34:7. He is about to argue that the Dutch Bibles of his time inserted the word "guilty" (schuldigen), which he believes distorts the original Hebrew meaning.</note> In this case, those words seem not to have been translated well, for the source language <note>original: "grond-taal," referring to Hebrew</note> does not contain the word "guilty." Rather, the translators—in order to make sense of the passage—have inserted it there. The reasons why this translation appears to be rejectable are these:

1. Because it seems to be a contradiction. If it has just been said that **The Lord** forgives iniquity, transgression, and sin, it would then follow in the same breath that **He** shall by no means hold the guilty innocent. This would immediately strip away all the previous comfort found in **God's Name**.

2. Because this does not agree with **God's** promise in **Exodus 33:19**: *I will make all my goodness pass before you.* That general promise is explained, opened up, and fulfilled in these two verses, **Exodus 34:6–7**.

3. Because this text, **Exodus 34:6–7**, does not speak of **God's** disposition and conduct toward His enemies, against whom He acts with great severity (just as **He** also commanded the **Israelites** to do in the following verses).

The first translation and explanation / which I indeed judge the most emphatic and easiest / is this. The words in the source language <note>original: "grond-taal," referring to the original Hebrew of the Old Testament</note> run thus: **Forgiving iniquity, transgression, and sin, and cleansing or in the cleansing He shall not cleanse (or in acquitting He shall not acquit)** <note>This reflects a literal translation of the Hebrew idiom *naqqeh lo yinaqqeh*, which uses a repeated verb for emphasis</note> **visiting the iniquity of the Fathers,** etc.; that is: cleansing or acquitting some of sins / He shall not cleanse or acquit all / but visit the iniquity of the Fathers, etc. **So that this Title of God’s Honorable Name / gives this to be known / God forgives whom He will / and upon whom He will / He visited the iniquity; that is / the Lord proves and exercises His mercy / grace / longsuffering / and abundant goodness toward Sinners / only according to the movements of His own will / on no other grounds / than because He wills / and toward no other Persons / and from no other respect / than only because / and to those whom He pleases He will be [so] / let them be as great or unworthy Sinners as they may be : and that is truly the very mystery of the Gospel: namely that all the Gospel dispensations are only toward those / to whom God will graciously do good / and on no other ground / than of God’s will and good pleasure :** <note>original: "welbehagen," a technical theological term for God's sovereign choice</note> just as Christ, thanking the Father for that / said / **yes Father, for so it was your good pleasure.** Matthew 11:26. It is indeed to all unworthy Sinners / that the Gospel is presented / and Christ offered / but to some according to His will and good pleasure and grace / and because He so wills: And so it is the same / which

# Nature and Foundations

...be courageous: for God has—beyond His word and promise (which by themselves ought to have been a sufficient foundation of trust for sinners who take refuge in Him through **Christ**)—added many confirmations, pledges, and seals to this end; **God** has sworn, swearing by Himself, and by His holiness, and as truly as He lives (for He had no greater one by whom to swear), that He will be a **God** to the believing covenant-partners <note>original: "bontgenooten"; those who are members of the formal agreement between God and man.</note> / a **God** of complete salvation: that He will surely bring them, through their faith, to the possession of the glorious inheritance: Thus He wished to give them a strong comfort, indeed an eternal comfort (as the Apostle calls it elsewhere) *2 Thessalonians 2: 16.* and good hope through grace: He wanted—says the text—to more abundantly prove and assure to the heirs of the promises, who have taken refuge to hold fast to the hope set before them, the immutability of His counsel; a counsel of peace / concerning the saving of sinners through **Christ**, accepted in faith: and therefore He intervened with an oath, to take away all doubt and suspicion <note>original: "suspici"</note>, for it is impossible that **God** should lie in two immutable things: His word and His oath; and the oath for confirmation is, even among men, an end of all contradiction *Hebrews 6: 16 / 18.* And as if an oath for the confirmation of this **Covenant** and for the reassurance of the believers <note>original: "geloobigen"</note> was not enough / the Lord has, for our assurance, also sworn to **Christ** / as the Head of all the believing covenant-partners / *I have sworn to my servant **David**, I will not li-* <note>The text ends mid-sentence on the catchword "lie," likely referencing Psalm 89:35, "I will not lie to David."</note>

# Of Faith

...partners. Psalm 89: 4 / 36 / 37. The Lord has sworn, and He will not repent: you are a Priest forever according to the order of *Melchizedek* Psalm 110: 4, *Hebrews* 7: 21. Should one then still doubt God’s promises, upon which He causes us to hope? A man is believed on his word, and much more so on his oath; if we receive the testimony of men, the testimony of God is greater 1 *John* 5: 9. And I add to this: the Oath of God is much greater still. Who then dares, through unbelief, to make Him out to be a liar, or even—it is a terrifying thought—a perjurer <note>original: "meineedige"; one who breaks a holy oath.</note>, as if He would swear falsely for the sake of humans (whom He has no need of)?

But we have yet another great seal of all the promises of the Covenant: *Christ’s* blood and death, which expressly serve to confirm the *Testament* <term>Testament: in this context, a legal will that only becomes valid and takes effect after the death of the person who made it.</term> of the promises. A *Testament* is made firm by the dead; no one among men doubts that. Brothers, says *Paul*, I speak in human terms: even a man’s covenant that is confirmed (his *Testament*, upon which he has died), no one sets aside, and no one adds to it, *Galatians* 3: verse 15. Shall *Christ* die to earn and leave behind the promised blessings, and thus confirm the covenant of grace as His eternal *Testament*—and shall we still doubt whether we, after we have embraced Him in faith, shall obtain the goods earned and left behind by *Testament*? Yet, because the Lord knew our weakness of faith <note>original: "zwakgeloovigheidt"</note> and our mistrust, He attached two more sensible <note>Meaning "perceivable by the senses."</note>, visible seals to this, His Covenant: so that through those signs and seals...

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 85 regarding the "seals" of God’s covenant. # Nature and Grounds

[...means] which we see with our eyes and touch with our hands, so that we might have full assurance; God set the rainbow before the world as a reassuring sign that He would not cause the world to perish by water again; and to Gideon He gave a sign for his confirmation regarding the truth of His promise; <note>Gideon, a judge of Israel, famously asked God for signs involving a fleece of wool to confirm he would lead Israel to victory (Judges 6).</note> so God has given **Baptism** as a sure and infallible sign and seal that believers shall be cleansed and saved, just as Noah and his household were saved in the Ark, floating upon the water while all others perished (1 Pet. 3: 20, 21). Moreover, He has given believers the **Sacrament of the Lord's Supper**, so that they might know with such certainty and assure themselves that they have communion <note>The Dutch "gemeinschap" implies a deep, shared participation or "fellowship."</note> in the fruit of Jesus' suffering and obedience, and in Jesus Christ Himself, and in the Covenant or Testament of the promises, as surely as they know that they are partakers of that bread and that wine of the Holy Supper; so that through these, as through divine seals, all the promises of the New Covenant are made so firm for those who accept Christ that no room for doubt is left; especially since they also have in their hearts an irrefutable pledge <term>underpant: a legal or financial guarantee or down payment that ensures the rest of a promise will be fulfilled</term> and confirming seal: the **Holy Spirit** of promise and of faith, who has sealed them for the Lord, after they believed, until the day of redemption; so that they can know and be able to know that they remain in Him and He in them, because He has given them of His Spirit (1 John 4: 13; 1 John 3: verse 24).

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "grounds" or foundations of faith. The previous page (85/86) concluded by discussing the "seals" of the covenant (Baptism and the Lord's Supper) and the internal sealing of the Holy Spirit. This page introduces the "ninth ground" for the believer's assurance.</meta>

->of Faith.<-

The ninth ground, for the assurance and confirmation of those who embrace **Christ** and heartily consent to Him as He is presented in the **Gospel** <note>original: "Euangelio"</note>, is: Because **Christ**, the **Son of God**, came into the world for them, became human, and as **Mediator** and **Surety** <term>Borg: a legal term for one who takes on the legal and financial obligations or debts of another to ensure they are paid</term> has suffered and made satisfaction in their place.

I join two things together here, which we nevertheless must note each distinctly: first, **Christ's** incarnation; and second, His suffering in that humanity.

Regarding the first; the most incomprehensible and unbelievable wonder that was ever in the world, the greatest work that **God** ever did or shall do, wherein **God’s** infinite wisdom, goodness, grace, and power shine most gloriously, is that the Word, which was in the beginning with **God** and was **God** Himself, became flesh and dwelt—or tabernacled <note>A reference to the Old Testament Tabernacle, signifying God's presence dwelling among His people.</note>—among us humans; that the only-begotten, own, and beloved **Son of God**, who is in the bosom of the **Father**, has taken on our nature (**John 1:1, 14, 18**). The **Father** prepared a body for Him (**Hebrews 10:5**). Him who is His companion <note>original: "medgesel," here referring to one who is an equal associate in the Godhead (Zechariah 13:7)</note> and His equal (**Philippians 2:6**). **God** was revealed in the flesh; that mystery of godliness <term>godzaligheyd: the profound nature of the divine plan and the piety it produces in believers</term> is beyond all doubt great (**1 Timothy 3:16**). The second person in the **Divine** essence (not the first, nor the third) has taken on human nature in the unity of His person. He became **Immanuel, God** with us, or **God** in our nature (**Isaiah 7:14**). He is like the children, flesh and blood

Out of brevity flowing from here / one should note three things / 1. For whom He prays. 2. What He requests for them. 3. Why that which He requests will surely be given.

First, note for whom He prays; it is for those who have been given to Him in the **covenant of redemption** <term>The eternal agreement within the Trinity where Christ agreed to save the elect.</term> and **suretyship** <term>A legal role where Christ takes responsibility for the spiritual debts of others.</term> / for thus He says: *I pray not for the world, but for those whom you have given me.* John 17:9. <note>original: "Joh. 17: 9."</note> Now these are His transgressors <note>The author refers to the elect as "His transgressors" to show that even those chosen for salvation were originally sinners in need of grace.</note> just like the others / and therefore it is written / Isaiah 53:12. <note>original: "Jes. 53: 12."</note> *He has prayed for the transgressors, borne the sins of many, and poured out his soul for them unto death;* so that His death and His intercession have an equally wide extent. Yet in particular it is written that He prayed for those who already believed / and not only for those / but also for those / who would believe in Him, John 17:20. <note>original: "Joh. 17: 20."</note> so that surely every believer has a share in Christ's intercession.

Secondly, note what Christ prays for the transgressors / whom He calls His own; In general, He prays for everything which / for the good of the **elect** <term>Those chosen by God for salvation before the foundation of the world.</term> / has been promised to Him in the covenant of redemption; He prays for all that His own have need of / and what has been promised to them in the **covenant of grace** <term>The historical administration of God's promise to save people through faith in Christ.</term>; and in particular / He prays that all the elect who have been given to Him / who are not yet **born again** <term>Regenerated; the spiritual rebirth necessary for salvation.</term> / nor yet believing / may be born again / and actually believe / so that in this way through His knowledge (that is, faith in that passage) many may be made righteous through Him. # 114 Nature and Grounds:

to embrace such sinners is engraved in such large letters that a person passing by can read them <note>This is a reference to the biblical imagery in Habakkuk 2:2, suggesting that God’s intent is made plain and easy to understand for everyone.</note>; indeed, our willingness is a fruit of His; we are not willing first, just as we do not love Him first; our willingness follows His and springs from His. He willed our will, in order to open the heart for Him. Why then should a believer who embraces Christ be any longer filled with doubt?

The fourteenth ground, for the advancement of full assurance and quiet confidence of those sinners who embrace Christ in their heart, just as He is presented and offered to them by the Father in the Gospel, is that it is the Father’s and Christ’s particular delight, joy, and pleasure to lead believing sinners through that path to glory. <meta>This page concludes the "fourteenth ground" of assurance and introduces the "fifteenth ground." The text continues from the previous page's discussion on the undeniable nature of the believer's hope.</meta>

->of Faith.<-

[un]deniability <note>The Dutch text begins with "segging," the second half of a word like "tegensegging" (contradiction or denial). Combined with the previous page's ending "but on...", it suggests the believer's state is "undeniable."</note> because his hope and trust are built upon such an **unshakable ground**.

The **fifteenth ground** of full assurance of faith and hope for a believing sinner is that **God** knows how to receive more honor by bringing a miserable, unworthy, and lost sinner to **salvation** <term>salvation</term> <note>Translated from *zaligheid*, meaning the state of eternal blessedness and deliverance from sin.</note>, in the way that He Himself invented, chosen, and revealed, than by any other means in the whole world.

This will be evident if we observe that there are **three ways** through which **God** can glorify Himself regarding rational creatures—humans or **angels**: 

1. By upholding them in the holy and blessed state in which they were created, so that in dependence upon Him, through obedience, they work out their eternal salvation. 

2. By letting out His **righteous wrath** and vengeance upon fallen humans and **angels**. 

3. By bringing lost sinners to **salvation** through His **grace** and love in **Christ**. 

The **first way** He took with those **angels** who, through His support, remained standing and persevered in obedience. The **second way** the **Lord** uses regarding the **fallen angels** and regarding those sinful humans who either hear and know nothing of **Christ**, or reject **Christ**, not wishing to be sincerely brought by Him to peace, holiness, and salvation. The **third way** the **Lord** has chosen regarding the **elect** <term>elect</term> <note>The *uitverkoorne* (the chosen) refers to those whom God, according to Reformed doctrine, has chosen from eternity to be saved through Christ.</note> humans who, in time, embrace **Christ** as a **Savior**.

<meta>catchword: Now</meta>

<summary>This page presents the fifteenth ground for spiritual assurance, arguing that God is most glorified not through simple obedience or punishment, but through the salvation of lost sinners via grace. It categorizes God's dealings with rational beings into three distinct paths: preservation, justice, and redemption.</summary>

<keywords>assurance of faith, salvation, glory of God, angels, righteous wrath, grace, the elect, Christ as Savior</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "fifteenth ground" of assurance, specifically detailing the "third way" God glorifies Himself: the salvation of sinners through grace. It follows the previous page's list of the three ways God receives honor from His creatures.</meta>

### Nature and Grounds

Now, through this last way, God knows how to glorify Himself more and to obtain more honor for Himself, not only by demonstrating His righteousness and holiness in eternally punishing the sins of Devils and reprobate <term>reprobate</term> <note>Those who are rejected by God and destined for damnation due to their persistent sin and lack of faith.</note> men, but also through the other Angels, who remained steadfast in grace through His strengthening manner. For the way of salvation for sinful men is through the intervention of the shedding of the blood of the Son of God; and therein is infinitely more divine love, grace, and loving-kindness than in confirming the Angels in a state of eternal holiness and blessedness. 

Here, God has—at a cost highest above all comprehension of men and Angels—chosen to pour out His infinite love and mercy toward lost Sinners, namely through the death of His own and only-begotten beloved Son. In this, God has shown His manifold wisdom more than in any single one of His works (Ephesians 3:10). 

Here, God has willed to express the infinite abyss of His free and pure grace toward unworthy, powerless, and utterly lost sinners, who were no better than those who are lost forever because of their sins. Therefore it is written that here we behold the glory of the Lord as in a mirror, namely in the face of Jesus Christ (2 Corinthians 3:18; 2 Corinthians 4:6), and that it is to God "for a name of joy, for a praise and for an honor," <note>original: "tot een vrolyken naam, tot een roem en tot een cieraat"</note> that He thus cleanses the Sinners from all their iniquities, and for-

### Nature and Grounds

Never can a **Sinner** honor **God** more, or ascribe more excellent praise and glory to Him, than by coming to **Him** through the **Savior Christ**, to receive all grace and glory from Him for **Christ’s** sake. When a convicted <note>In this context, "convicted" (overtuigt) refers to a person who has become deeply aware of their own spiritual needs or "sinfulness."</note> **Sinner** stands heart-amazed at the immensity and unfathomability of **God’s** free grace, when the **Gospel** is presented, and sees the height, depth, length, and breadth of **Christ’s** love, which surpasses all knowledge—and in contemplating this, worships **God**, and then turns his soul toward it, and builds upon **God** in **Christ**, and embraces **God’s** love and grace offered in **Christ**, resting upon it—then **God** the **Lord** receives, in an incomprehensibly high manner, the praise and glory of His grace and love through such a soul. For **God** has so arranged everything in His dealings with sinful men that all shall tend and result in the praise of the glory of His grace, as **Paul** says in **Ephesians 1:6.** <note>The verse referenced reads: "To the praise of the glory of his grace, wherein he hath made us accepted in the beloved."</note>

In this and through this, **God** has willed to make Himself glorious for **Angels** and men in the most special way; and **He** has willed that all the praise, fame, glory, and service which **He** wishes to receive from anyone in this world, should come to **Him** by the way of His grace, love, and wisdom, in bringing **Sinners** through **Christ** to glory; just as glory and praise shall be attributed to Him for eternity before **Angels** and **Men**.

Therefore, all believing sinners may and must come boldly in full assurance and confidence of faith to the throne of grace, because they have there a great **High Prie-** <meta>catchword: ster (priest)</meta>

## Nature and Grounds [of Faith]

It effects <term>conversion<note>original: "bekeering." The process of turning one’s life toward God.</note></term> and <term>rebirth<note>original: "wedergeboorte." Even the Angels could not conceive of the way through which sinful men would be brought to salvation; and when the way and the means, devised by God, were revealed to them, they saw a <term>manifold wisdom<note>original: "veelvuldige wysheidt." <meta>This page concludes the argument regarding personal spiritual insight and begins a list of instructions on the nature of spiritual assurance. It transitions from the medical metaphor of the previous page—where the soul's "wound" is treated by Christ—to correcting misconceptions about the "feeling" of being saved.</meta>

# Nature and Grounds

...that which is of **God** can see it. I have had to present this at such length because many are held back by these entangling thoughts from going directly and immediately in **faith** to **Christ** when they see their misery and wish to come to Him.

**Twelfth.** Beware of imagining and believing that the spiritual **assurance** <term>verzekertheid</term> <note>The internal certainty of one's salvation and standing before God.</note>, which you can obtain by contemplating the inward signs of **grace** and through the aforementioned grounds of comfort and full **Assurance**, with the help of the **Holy Spirit**, is a kind of quiet, uncontested tranquility and certainty of the soul, by which one would be in full and sensible joy, triumph, shouting, and thanksgiving at all times; to have such a wrong **Imagination** is particularly harmful: rarely or never will they come to assurance who have such a wrong conception of the nature and state of assurance and firm trust; therefore, note the following things.

1. A soul that is well assured on good grounds can simultaneously be under a deep feeling of the evil of **sin**; she can feel the guilt of the sin committed, being clearly convinced of it by the **Holy Spirit**, and feeling that, according to the sentence of the **Law**, she has deserved wrath thereby; and notwithstanding this, she can look upon **God** as her **God**, and see through **faith** that she has a personal share and right in the **Evangelical acquittal** <term>Euangelische vryspreking</term> <note>The theological concept of justification, where a believer is declared righteous through the Gospel rather than their own merits.</note>; she can loathe herself <note>The original Dutch text reads "verfoepen," which is likely a typo for "verfoeien" (to loathe or detest).</note> for her ingratitude and rebelliousness, and yet her assurance remains, as is evident in **David**, **Psalm 31: verses 23 and 15**.

<summary>This page addresses the common misconception that spiritual assurance always feels like constant joy, explaining that a believer can simultaneously feel the weight of their sins and the certainty of God's forgiveness. It emphasizes that true assurance is compatible with deep repentance and a sense of guilt.</summary>
<keywords>assurance, grace, Holy Spirit, sin, justification, David, Psalm 31, repentance, faith</keywords>

--- Page 193 ---
<meta>This page continues a numbered list of clarifications regarding "Evangelical assurance." The previous page established that assurance is not a state of constant, ecstatic joy, but can coexist with a sense of guilt under the Law. This page (points two through four) further explains how certainty of salvation often increases one's grief over sin and awareness of internal corruption.</meta>

# ... of Faith.

**Secondly.** Evangelical assurance <term>Evangelischs versekertheidt</term> <note>The internal certainty of being saved through the Gospel of Christ rather than through one’s own works.</note> does not exclude godly sorrow over sins, but it can certainly coexist with manifold and deep sorrow over the same. If someone thinks they have assurance of their well-being and yet ordinarily lacks the "sorrow after God" <note>original: "droefheidt na Godt"; a reference to the "godly sorrow" mentioned in 2 Corinthians 7:10 which leads to repentance.</note> over their transgressions, then they have reason to be wary of their assurance: for blessed are those who mourn, for they shall be comforted, Matthew 5: 4. Toward such a person will He look, and those He will revive, Isaiah 66: 2; Isaiah 57: 15. It is one of the promises of the covenant of grace <term>genade verbond</term> <note>The theological framework where God promises eternal life to believers based on the sacrifice of Jesus.</note> that one shall bitterly lament over one’s sins, Zechariah 12: 16 <note>The original text cites verse 16, though the prophet Zechariah only has 14 verses; this likely refers to the mourning described in Zechariah 12:10-12.</note>. Indeed, one might well say that the more someone is assured of forgiveness, the more they will be sorrowful over their sins, Luke 7: 37, 38, 47, 48.

**Thirdly.** An assured soul can and will have a deep feeling of the indwelling restless power of sin, as was evident in Paul, who was exceedingly sensitive to the inherent and active corruption <term>inklevende de werkzaame verdorvenheid</term> <note>A term for the "remnants" of the sinful nature that theologians believe remain in a person even after they become a Christian.</note>, Romans 7: 14—24; and yet at the same time could say: *I thank God through Jesus Christ our Lord, there is no condemnation for those who are in Christ*, Romans 7: 25; Romans 8: 1.

**Fourthly.** Where assurance is present, there can be at the same time—indeed, there is at the same time—a self-condemnation and self-loathing because of the sin that one sees and finds in oneself; Job said, *Therefore I loathe myself, and repent in dust and ashes*, Job 42: 6. If we judge ourselves, says Paul, then we shall not be judged by the Lord, 1 Corinthians 11: 31.

<sig>L 5</sig>
<meta>catchword: Ten</meta>

<summary>This page argues that true spiritual assurance is paradoxically linked to a deep, ongoing sorrow for sin and a recognition of one's own corruption. It uses the examples of Paul and Job to show that feeling "saved" does not mean feeling "sinless" or untroubled.</summary>
<keywords>Evangelical assurance, godly sorrow, covenant of grace, indwelling sin, self-condemnation, Apostle Paul, Job, repentance</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the numbered list of characteristics of spiritual assurance, specifically how it interacts with doubt and fluctuating emotions. It picks up from point four, which was likely on the previous page, and covers points five through seven.</meta>

# Nature and Grounds
-> L 2 <-

<margin>Psalm 31:15, 23</margin>
**Fifthly.** It cannot be otherwise but that where there is assurance <term>verzekertheidt</term> <note>A term often used in Reformed theology to describe the internal certainty of one's salvation.</note> of being in a state of grace <term>staat der genade</term>, there also remains a struggle against doubts and temptations to doubt. It is incomprehensible that an assured soul would not harbor within itself contrary reasonings and objections against its certain trust. The reason is that within us there are remnants of unbelief just as there are of any other sins. Faith is not perfect here, and Satan cannot and will not refrain from attacking assured souls. Yet this is also true: that an assured person ordinarily overcomes those doubts and does not remain succumb to them. Nevertheless, they are still subject to attacks of doubt; our assurance is not such a high tranquility of trust that one would have no struggle with it, nor sudden doubts, just as David experienced. <note>The author references David to show that even a "man after God's own heart" faced moments of deep uncertainty.</note>

**Sixthly.** The assurance of God’s children on earth does not always stand at the highest peak; it is sometimes higher, sometimes lower, sometimes greater, sometimes obscured, sometimes accompanied by more joy; sometimes with much sensible comfort <note>Comfort that can be physically or emotionally felt.</note> and triumphant joy, sometimes with less feeling. Even if one lacks the feeling of peace and forgiveness, of joy and rest, one can still remain assured through the exercise of faith <term>oeffening des geloofs</term> <note>The theological concept that faith is a continuous activity or "work" of the soul, rather than a static emotion.</note>.

**Seventhly.** Although assurance is accompanied by all this [fluctuation] even in the best of people, it is not therefore to be lightly esteemed. For as much as it fills us with comfort and joy, it takes away tormenting fear, since one knows... <note>The text ends mid-sentence, continuing on the next page.</note>

<summary>This page argues that spiritual assurance is not a state of constant emotional high or a lack of doubt, but a resilient faith that persists through internal conflict and fluctuating feelings.</summary>
<keywords>assurance of grace, spiritual doubt, remnants of sin, exercise of faith, David, spiritual struggle, comfort and joy</keywords>

# of Faith.

...one will not be put to shame. <margin>1 John 4:18.</margin> <margin>Romans 5:2–5.</margin> It <note>Referring to spiritual assurance.</note> supports and strengthens the soul for and in suffering; it is the precious means to make the life of holiness flourish in us; it promotes obedience, indeed, it causes one to obey cheerfully, since the Spirit of God bears witness to the soul, sweetly shining upon and sealing it.

### Thirteenthly.

Do not attempt to make your godliness <term>godtzaligheidt</term> and holiness a part of your foundation upon which you would rest your soul in the expectation of being accepted by God and ensuring yourself of your state. This has hindered many for a long time from attaining peace and assurance: that they first wanted to make themselves acceptable through sanctification <term>heiligmaking</term>, the performance of duties, and the mortification <term>dooding</term> <note>The practice of "killing off" sinful impulses through spiritual discipline.</note> of sin—not entirely with the exclusion of Christ and free grace, but in part. 

As a result, they have wanted to build their foundation of trust and rest upon two things: first, God's grace in Christ, and then their holy endeavors and progress in the fruitfulness of good works. This is clearly evident because they dare not let the weight of the soul's salvation rest solely upon Christ and upon the grace in and through Him, fearing they might deceive themselves and be put to shame. Thus, they want to make the foundation firm with their own holiness, obedience, and good works, so that it might not fail. By doing this, we significantly wrong Christ and His grace; and the Lord never allows them to succeed in this, so that they are continually in unrest, fear, and
->anxi-<- <note>The catchword "enst-" likely completes the word "angst," meaning anxiety or fear.</note>

# Nature and Grounds

...live in uncertainty, because the foundation cannot be made firmer by their good endeavors. Therefore, be very careful not to make any ground, or wish to make ground, out of holiness. Instead, lay your foundation purely and solely upon Christ and His mediatorial work <term>Middelaars werk</term> <note>The theological concept of Christ acting as the bridge or "middle-man" between God and humanity.</note>, upon God's sheer grace and mercy, and upon the covenant of grace. Accept these upon the offer of the Gospel, and rest so singly upon grace, expecting salvation and all that can lead to it; for out of grace <margin>Ephesians 2:8–9.</margin> one is saved through faith. <margin>Romans 3:24–25.</margin> Works have no place there to create the ground of trust—not in the least. Rather, it is the grace given to us through Christ, as Paul <margin>2 Timothy 1:9.</margin> makes known.

But having ventured <term>gestout</term> <note>From the Dutch 'stouten,' meaning to dare or to place the full weight of one's hope upon something.</note> your soul for salvation upon that single foundation that is laid—besides which no other foundation can be laid, even in part, as the <margin>1 Corinthians 3:11.</margin> Apostle says—strive then to give all obedience in Christ’s strength and out of love for Him. ### of Faith.

...apply themselves, according to the counsel and guidance of the Lord and His ministers, to use the **means of assurance**, to believe in Christ, to watch, to pray, and to fight against corruptions. Many have remained without **assurance** <note>The term "assurance" (verzekerdheid) was a central concern of the Dutch Second Reformation; it refers to the subjective certainty a believer feels regarding their salvation.</note> for months and years, because they did nothing but complain about their comfortless state, and did little or nothing toward deliverance from that state: To this end, I must say the following things.

**First.** There are some who complain about their state, regarding whom one may rightly doubt whether they are truly earnest about the work—indeed, whether they have any true grace at all: for they maintain this character. They complain everywhere they have the opportunity that they are unassured, whether they are converted, and whether they have a part in Christ; that they fear they shall be lost; but they are not active in the use of the **means** <note>In this context, "means" (middelen) refers to the "Means of Grace," such as prayer, Bible study, and attending sermons, which were believed to be the tools God uses to strengthen faith.</note>; they do not watch over their conduct; they neglect many duties; they are lazy and sluggish; they do not wish to exert themselves much; they love **fleshly ease**; they do not crucify their sins; they give themselves over to anger, passion, pride, vanity, and laziness. All they have is their complaining: they are in a bad state, although they often do not name—nor can they name—where exactly the fault lies, or what hinders their peace: so that they cannot come to any assurance of their share in Jesus. They delay various people with their complaints: they learn to make objections against themselves: but whatever is said, they remain just as death-like, dull, and inactive; fundamentally, they have some imagination that they have something good in them...

# Nature and Foundations

Verses 38, 41, 48. It is a mistaken and foolish notion that true godly sorrow <note>Original: "droefheidt na Godt." This refers to the "godly sorrow" described in 2 Corinthians 7:10, which leads to repentance, as opposed to "worldly sorrow" which leads to death.</note> flows from a lack of the joy of faith; on the contrary, the soul that is most comforted and filled by the faithful acceptance and application of **Christ** and the promises, is the one that mourns the most. This is because such a soul sees sin more clearly and in a spiritual manner. The less one applies **Christ** through faith, the less clearly and spiritually one perceives sin; consequently, one does not mourn for unseemly conduct in such an evangelical <note>In this context, "evangelical" (Evangelische) refers to a motivation arising from the Gospel of grace rather than from a legalistic fear of punishment.</note> or abundant way.

4. You shall not fight any less against sin, nor be less diligent in holy duties, when you frequently apply **Christ** for your comfort and cheerfulness. For that joy of faith, being the joy of the **Lord**, shall be your strength (Nehemiah 8:10). You will hold higher esteem and more precious thoughts of the **Lord**, and thus you will fear all the more to offend Him, and be more jealous and watchful so as to do nothing against His love and grace. You will also have less self-confidence and depend less on yourself regarding comfort and holiness, as you continually gaze with longing <note>Original: "gappende." An archaic Dutch term literally meaning "gaping" or "panting," used here to describe a soul hungering for and looking expectantly toward Christ.</note> and joy toward **Christ** and the fullness that is in **Christ** and in the covenant; and so you will do more work for the **Lord**.

->of Faith<-

...speak to and of individual souls; they believe that this depends on the individual **Sinner’s** condition—that they must first see some holy quality in their soul to which the **Lord** has attached promises of salvation and acceptance, such as those found in **Matthew 3:3–9**. <note>Matthew 3:3–9 describes John the Baptist calling for "fruit in keeping with repentance" and warns that the axe is at the root of the trees. The author argues that some teachers wrongly use this to suggest one must see this "fruit" before they are allowed to trust in Christ.</note> All this reasoning stems from an unwillingness to categorize the direct exercise of faith in **Christ** as one of the signs of salvation. Indeed, by doing so, they exclude **faith**—which is the most vital and essential sign of all—from the marks that should bring a **Sinner** to the assurance of their standing before **God**.

However, the following points will show how mistaken this view is:

**1.** It is impossible for a soul to have any share in any promise before it has reached out in **faith** toward **Jesus** to be united with Him. **Jesus** is the primary promise and the foundation of all others (**Genesis 3:15**) <note>The Dutch text cites Gen. 3:16, but likely refers to verse 15, known as the *Protevangelium* or the first gospel promise regarding the seed of the woman.</note>, and **faith** has **Jesus** as its chief object. Furthermore, it is impossible to conceive of any condition, grace, or holy quality within the soul that could be pleasing to **God** *prior* to faith and union with **Christ**. For either that quality flows from **Christ** or it does not. If it does not, then not all grace comes out of His fullness, which contradicts **John 1:16** and **Colossians 1:19**. <note>original: "Joh. 1: vers 16. Coloss. 1: vers 19." These verses emphasize that all spiritual blessings and the fullness of God dwell in Christ alone.</note> If it *does* flow from Him, then a union must have first been established through **faith**. After all, the communion <term>Communion: The sharing or "communication" of spiritual benefits from Christ to the believer.</term> or sharing of **Christ’s** graces with a soul presupposes a union and follows from it.

**2.** If the inviting promises <term>Inviting promises: General offers of the Gospel that invite everyone to come to Christ, regardless of their current state.</term>, which **God**...

# Nature and Grounds

...faith of assurance, as knowing in whom one has believed, and to whom and upon what words of promise one has entrusted his soul; to which this is further added.

4. All the holy qualities or workings to which promises are attached in the Word (take, for example, those found in Matthew 5:3–9 <note>This refers to the Beatitudes, such as "Blessed are the poor in spirit" and "Blessed are the meek."</note>) obtain their favor with God only from the fact that they proceed from the union that is made with **Christ** through faith, and because they are accompanied by the exercise of faith. Thus, one can say with truth in a proper sense that faith alone is the sign for the assurance of a soul that it has been accepted and will be saved. For faith alone brings the soul into union with **Christ**—not love, nor humility, uprightness, meekness, mercy, or peacefulness—and faith alone is the condition of the covenant of grace <term>covenant of grace: the theological arrangement where God provides salvation as a free gift through faith in Jesus Christ, rather than through human merit or law-keeping</term>; faith alone is the great command of the **Gospel**; and all other signs must be brought to these signs of faith and be tested by them. Furthermore, they receive their nourishment and growth by means of the same; if poverty of spirit, meekness, hunger and thirst for righteousness, mercy, and peacefulness were viewed without the exercise of faith, they would not be signs that provide assurance; so it is also with love, goodness, temperance, etc. Thus, it must be said: the great, essential, foundational sign, on which all signs depend and to which they must be referred, is faith in the Lord Jesus.

<summary>The author argues that while many virtues are praised in the Bible, they only provide true assurance of salvation when they spring from a primary union with Christ through faith. Faith is presented as the foundational "sign" and the sole condition of the covenant of grace.</summary>
